					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content"><div class="mw-subjectpageheader">
</div><div class="mw-content-ltr mw-parser-output" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Organized collection of books or other information resources</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1320445320">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}@media print{body.ns-0 .mw-parser-output .hatnote{display:none!important}}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Library_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Library (disambiguation)">Library (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:SanDiegoCityCollegeLearningResource_-_bookshelf.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/SanDiegoCityCollegeLearningResource_-_bookshelf.jpg/250px-SanDiegoCityCollegeLearningResource_-_bookshelf.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="188" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/SanDiegoCityCollegeLearningResource_-_bookshelf.jpg/500px-SanDiegoCityCollegeLearningResource_-_bookshelf.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="960" /></a><figcaption>A library patron retrieving a book from a shelf at a library</figcaption></figure>
<p>A <b>library</b> is a collection of <a href="/wiki/Book" title="Book">books</a>, and possibly other <a href="/wiki/Document" title="Document">materials</a> and <a href="/wiki/Media_(communication)" class="mw-redirect" title="Media (communication)">media</a>, that is accessible for use by its members and members of allied institutions. Libraries provide physical (hard copies) or <a href="/wiki/Electronic_media" title="Electronic media">digital</a> (soft copies) materials, and may be a physical location, a virtual space, or both. A library's collection normally includes <a href="/wiki/Printed" class="mw-redirect" title="Printed">printed</a> materials which can be borrowed, and usually also includes a reference section of publications which may only be utilized inside the premises. Resources such as commercial releases of films, television programmes, other video recordings, radio, music and audio recordings may be available in many formats. These include <a href="/wiki/DVD" title="DVD">DVDs</a>, <a href="/wiki/Blu-ray" title="Blu-ray">Blu-rays</a>, <a href="/wiki/CD" class="mw-redirect" title="CD">CDs</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cassette_tape" title="Cassette tape">cassettes</a>, or other applicable formats such as <a href="/wiki/Microform" title="Microform">microform</a>. They may also provide access to information, music or other content held on <a href="/wiki/Bibliographic_database" title="Bibliographic database">bibliographic databases</a>. In addition, some libraries offer <a href="/wiki/Library_makerspace" title="Library makerspace">creation stations</a> for <a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/maker" class="extiw" title="wiktionary:maker">makers</a> which offer access to a <a href="/wiki/3D_printing" title="3D printing">3D printing</a> station with a <a href="/wiki/3D_scanning" title="3D scanning">3D scanner</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>1<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Libraries can vary widely in size and may be organised and maintained by a public body such as a government, an institution (such as a school or <a href="/wiki/Museum" title="Museum">museum</a>), a corporation, or a private individual. In addition to providing materials, libraries also provide the services of <a href="/wiki/Librarian" title="Librarian">librarians</a> who are trained experts in finding, selecting, circulating and organising information while interpreting information needs and navigating and analysing large amounts of information with a variety of resources. The area of study is known as <a href="/wiki/Library_and_information_science" title="Library and information science">library and information science</a> or studies.
</p><p>Library buildings often provide quiet areas for studying, as well as common areas for group study and collaboration, and may provide public facilities for access to their electronic resources, such as computers and access to the <a href="/wiki/Internet" title="Internet">Internet</a>.
</p><p>The library's clientele and general services offered vary depending on its type, size and sometimes location: users of a <a href="/wiki/Public_library" title="Public library">public library</a> have different needs from those of a <a href="/wiki/Special_library" title="Special library">special library</a> or academic library, for example. Libraries may also be community hubs, where programmes are made available and people engage in lifelong learning. Modern libraries extend their services beyond the physical walls of the building by providing material accessible by electronic means, including from home via the Internet.
</p><p>The services that libraries offer are variously described as library services, information services, or the combination "library and information services", although different institutions and sources define such terminology differently.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Etymology">Etymology</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Etymology"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>The term <i>library</i> is based on the <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> word <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">liber</i></span> for 'book' or 'document', contained in Latin <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">libraria</i></span> 'collection of books' and <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">librarium</i></span> 'container for books'. Other modern languages use derivations from <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greek" title="Ancient Greek">Ancient Greek</a> <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">βιβλιοθήκη</span></span> (<span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language romanization"><i lang="grc-Latn">bibliothēkē</i></span>), originally meaning 'book container', via Latin <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">bibliotheca</i></span> (<a href="/wiki/Cf." title="Cf.">cf.</a> <a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a> <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">bibliothèque</i></span> or <a href="/wiki/German_language" title="German language">German</a> <span title="German-language text"><i lang="de">Bibliothek</i></span>).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>2<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="History">History</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: History"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_libraries" title="History of libraries">History of libraries</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Library_of_Ashurbanipal.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Library_of_Ashurbanipal.jpg/250px-Library_of_Ashurbanipal.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="141" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Library_of_Ashurbanipal.jpg/500px-Library_of_Ashurbanipal.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="4608" data-file-height="2592" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Library_of_Ashurbanipal" title="Library of Ashurbanipal">Library of Ashurbanipal</a> in <a href="/wiki/Mesopotamia" title="Mesopotamia">Mesopotamia</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1500–539 BC</span>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/History_of_libraries" title="History of libraries">history of libraries</a> began with the first efforts to organize collections of documents.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-britannica&#95;3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-britannica-3"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>3<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The first libraries consisted of <a href="/wiki/Archive" title="Archive">archives</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Writing#The_beginning_of_writing" title="Writing">earliest form of writing</a>—the <a href="/wiki/Clay_tablet" title="Clay tablet">clay tablets</a> in <a href="/wiki/Cuneiform_script" class="mw-redirect" title="Cuneiform script">cuneiform script</a> discovered in <a href="/wiki/Sumer" title="Sumer">Sumer</a>, some dating back to 2600 BC. Private or personal libraries made up of written books appeared in <a href="/wiki/Classical_Greece" title="Classical Greece">classical Greece</a> in the 5th century BC. In the 6th century, at the very close of the <a href="/wiki/Classical_antiquity" title="Classical antiquity">Classical period</a>, the great libraries of the Mediterranean world remained those of <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Library_of_Constantinople" title="Imperial Library of Constantinople">Constantinople</a> and <a href="/wiki/Library_of_Alexandria" title="Library of Alexandria">Alexandria</a>.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Fatimid_Caliphate" title="Fatimid Caliphate">Fatimids</a> (r. 909–1171) also possessed many great libraries within their domains. The historian <a href="/wiki/Ibn_Abi_Tayyi" title="Ibn Abi Tayyi">Ibn Abi Tayyi’</a> describes their palace library, which probably contained the largest collection of literature on earth at the time, as a "<a href="/wiki/Wonders_of_the_World" title="Wonders of the World">wonder of the world</a>". Throughout history, along with bloody massacres, the destruction of libraries has been critical for conquerors who wish to destroy every trace of the vanquished community's recorded memory. A prominent example of this can be found in the <a href="/wiki/Mongol_campaign_against_the_Nizaris" title="Mongol campaign against the Nizaris">Mongol massacre of the Nizaris</a> at <a href="/wiki/Alamut" title="Alamut">Alamut</a> in 1256 and the torching of their library, "the fame of which", boasts the conqueror Juwayni, "had spread throughout the world".<sup id="cite&#95;ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>4<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The libraries of <a href="/wiki/Timbuktu" title="Timbuktu">Timbuktu</a> were established in the fourteenth century and attracted scholars from all over the world.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>5<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The oldest modern public library was the <a href="/wiki/Za%C5%82uski_Library" title="Załuski Library">Załuski Library</a> (<a href="/wiki/Polish_language" title="Polish language">Polish</a>: <i lang="pl">Biblioteka Załuskich</i>, <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Bibliotheca Zalusciana</i>) established in 1732 in <a href="/wiki/Warsaw" title="Warsaw">Warsaw</a>, <a href="/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth" title="Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth">Poland-Lithuania</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>Drukarnia Pijarów 1<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> which ultimately evolved into the <a href="/wiki/National_Library_of_Poland" title="National Library of Poland">National Library of Poland</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Functions">Functions</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Functions"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1273380762/mw-parser-output/.tmulti">.mw-parser-output .tmulti .multiimageinner{display:flex;flex-direction:column}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .theader{clear:both;font-weight:bold;text-align:center;align-self:center;background-color:transparent;width:100%}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbcaption{background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output data-file-width="864" data-file-height="648" /></a></span></div></div><div class="tsingle" style="width:226px;max-width:226px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:120px;overflow:hidden"><span srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Activity_at_the_library5.png/500px-Activity_at_the_library5.png 1.5x" data-file-width="545" data-file-height="293" /></a></span></div></div></div><div class="trow" style="display:flex"><div class="thumbcaption">1. DVD collection at library; 2. Reading activity at the library</div></div></div></div>
<p>Libraries may provide physical or digital access to material, and may be a physical location, virtual space, or both (hybrid). A library's collection can include <a href="/wiki/Book" title="Book">books</a>, <a href="/wiki/Periodical_literature" title="Periodical literature">periodicals</a>, <a href="/wiki/Newspaper" title="Newspaper">newspapers</a>, <a href="/wiki/Manuscript" title="Manuscript">manuscripts</a>, <a href="/wiki/Film" title="Film">films</a>, <a href="/wiki/Map_collection" title="Map collection">maps</a>, <a href="/wiki/Print_room" title="Print room">prints</a>, <a href="/wiki/Document" title="Document">documents</a>, <a href="/wiki/Microform" title="Microform">microform</a>, <a href="/wiki/Compact_disc" title="Compact disc">CDs</a>, <a href="/wiki/Compact_Cassette" class="mw-redirect" title="Compact Cassette">cassettes</a>, <a href="/wiki/Videotape" title="Videotape">videotapes</a>, <a href="/wiki/DVD" title="DVD">DVDs</a>, <a href="/wiki/Blu-ray" title="Blu-ray">Blu-ray Discs</a>, <a href="/wiki/E-book" class="mw-redirect" title="E-book">e-books</a>, <a href="/wiki/Audiobook" title="Audiobook">audiobooks</a>, <a href="/wiki/Database" title="Database">databases</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tabletop_game" title="Tabletop game">table games</a>, <a href="/wiki/Video_game" title="Video game">video games</a>, and other formats. Libraries range widely in size, up to millions of items.
</p><p>Libraries often provide quiet spaces for private studying, common areas to facilitate group study and collaboration, and public facilities for access to their electronic resources and the <a href="/wiki/Internet" title="Internet">Internet</a>. Public and institutional collections and services may be intended for use by people who choose not to—or cannot afford to—purchase an extensive collection themselves, who need material no individual can reasonably be expected to have, or who require professional assistance with their research.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>6<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Services offered by a library are variously described as library services, information services, or the combination "library and information services", although different institutions and sources define such terminology differently. Organizations or departments are often called by one of these names.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>7<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>8<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>9<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>10<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>11<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>12<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>13<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Internal_organization">Internal organization</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Internal organization"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:HK_Wan_Chai_Library_Inside_Bookcase_a.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/HK_Wan_Chai_Library_Inside_Bookcase_a.jpg/250px-HK_Wan_Chai_Library_Inside_Bookcase_a.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="188" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/HK_Wan_Chai_Library_Inside_Bookcase_a.jpg/500px-HK_Wan_Chai_Library_Inside_Bookcase_a.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a><figcaption>Library shelves in <a href="/wiki/Hong_Kong" title="Hong Kong">Hong Kong</a>, showing numbers of the classification scheme to help readers locate works in that section</figcaption></figure>
<p>Most libraries have materials arranged in a specified order according to a <a href="/wiki/Library_classification" title="Library classification">library classification</a> system, so that items may be located quickly and collections browsed efficiently.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>14<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Some libraries have additional galleries beyond the public ones, where "reference" materials are stored. These reference stacks may be open to selected members of the public while others may require patrons to submit a "stack request" –  a request for an assistant to retrieve the material from the closed stacks: see <a href="/wiki/List_of_closed_stack_libraries" title="List of closed stack libraries">List of closed stack libraries</a>.
</p><p>Larger libraries are often divided into departments staffed by both paraprofessionals and professional librarians. Their department names and occupational designations may change depending on their location and the needs of the library.
</p>
<ul><li>Circulation (or Access Services/Stacks Maintenance) – Handles user accounts and the loaning/returning and shelving of materials.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>15<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Collection_development" title="Collection development">Collection Development</a> – Orders materials and maintains materials budgets.</li>
<li>Reference – Staffs a <a href="/wiki/Reference_desk" title="Reference desk">reference desk</a> answering questions from users (using structured <a href="/wiki/Reference_interview" title="Reference interview">reference interviews</a>), instructing users, and developing library programming. Reference may be further broken down by user groups or materials; common collections are <a href="/wiki/Children%27s_literature" title="Children&#39;s literature">children's literature</a>, <a href="/wiki/Young_adult_fiction" class="mw-redirect" title="Young adult fiction">young adult literature</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Genealogy" title="Genealogy">genealogy</a> materials.</li>
<li>Electronic Library – Responsible for providing information to users via electronic means.</li>
<li>Technical Services – Works behind the scenes cataloging and processing new materials and deaccessioning <a href="/wiki/Weeding_(library)" title="Weeding (library)">weeded materials</a>.</li></ul>
<p>Basic tasks in <a href="/wiki/Library_management" title="Library management">library management</a> include planning acquisitions (which materials the library should acquire, by purchase or otherwise), classifying and preserving items (especially rare and fragile archival materials such as manuscripts), deaccessioning materials, patron borrowing, and developing and administering library computer systems and technology.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>16<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> More long-term issues include planning the construction of new libraries or extensions to existing ones, and the development and implementation of outreach services and reading-enhancement services (such as adult literacy and children's programming). Library materials like books, magazines, periodicals, CDs, etc. are managed using a library classification system such as the <a href="/wiki/Dewey_Decimal_Classification" title="Dewey Decimal Classification">Dewey Decimal</a> Classification Theory, though libraries will usually adjust their classification system to fit their needs.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>17<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
The <a href="/wiki/International_Organization_for_Standardization" title="International Organization for Standardization">International Organization for Standardization</a> (ISO) has published several standards regarding the management of libraries through its Technical Committee 46 (TC 46),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>18<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> which is focused on "libraries, documentation and information centers, publishing, archives, records management, museum documentation, indexing and abstracting services, and information science". The following is a partial list of some of them:<sup id="cite&#95;ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>19<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>ISO 2789:2006 Information and documentation—International library statistics</li>
<li>ISO 11620:1998 Information and documentation—Library performance indicators</li>
<li>ISO 11799:2003 Information and documentation—Document storage requirements for archive and library materials</li>
<li>ISO 14416:2003 Information and documentation—Requirements for binding of books, periodicals, serials, and other paper documents for archive and library use—Methods and materials</li>
<li>ISO/TR 20983:2003 Information and documentation—Performance indicators for electronic library services</li></ul>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Usage">Usage</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Usage"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:2009_3962573662_card_catalog.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/2009_3962573662_card_catalog.jpg/250px-2009_3962573662_card_catalog.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="250" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/2009_3962573662_card_catalog.jpg/500px-2009_3962573662_card_catalog.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="2448" data-file-height="2448" /></a><figcaption>Until the advent of digital catalogues, <a href="/wiki/Library_catalog" title="Library catalog">card catalogues</a> were the traditional method of organizing the list of resources and their location within a large library.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Some patrons may not know how to fully utilize library resources, or feel unease in approaching a staff member. Ways in which a library's content is displayed or accessed may have an impact on use. An antiquated or clumsy search system, or staff unwilling or not properly trained to engage their patrons, will limit a library's usefulness. In the <a href="/wiki/Public_library" title="Public library">public libraries</a> of the United States, beginning in the 19th century, these problems drove the emergence of the <a href="/wiki/Library_instruction" title="Library instruction">library instruction</a> movement, which advocated library user education.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>20<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> One of the early leaders was <a href="/wiki/John_Cotton_Dana" title="John Cotton Dana">John Cotton Dana</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>21<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The basic form of library instruction is sometimes known as <a href="/wiki/Information_literacy" title="Information literacy">information literacy</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>22<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Libraries should inform their users of what materials are available in their collections and how to access that information. Before the computer age, this was accomplished by the card <a href="/wiki/Library_catalog" title="Library catalog">catalogue</a>—a cabinet (or multiple cabinets) containing many drawers filled with <a href="/wiki/Index_card" title="Index card">index cards</a> that identified books and other materials. In a large library, the card catalogue often filled a large room.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>23<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The emergence of <a href="/wiki/Desktop_computer" title="Desktop computer">desktop computers</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Internet" title="Internet">Internet</a>, however, has led to the adoption of electronic catalogue databases (often referred to as "webcats" or as <a href="/wiki/Online_public_access_catalog" title="Online public access catalog">online public access catalogues</a>, OPACs), which allow users to search the library's holdings from any location with Internet access.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>24<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> This style of catalogue maintenance is compatible with new types of libraries, such as <a href="/wiki/Digital_library" title="Digital library">digital libraries</a> and <a href="/wiki/Distributed_library" title="Distributed library">distributed libraries</a>, as well as older libraries that have been <a href="/wiki/Retrofitting" title="Retrofitting">retrofitted</a>. Large libraries may be scattered within multiple buildings across a town, each having multiple floors, with multiple rooms housing their resources across a series of shelves called <a href="/wiki/Bay_(shelving)" title="Bay (shelving)">bays</a>. Once a user has located a resource within the catalogue, they must then use navigational guidance to retrieve the resource physically, a process that may be assisted through signage, maps, <a href="/wiki/GPS" class="mw-redirect" title="GPS">GPS</a> systems, or <a href="/wiki/RFID" class="mw-redirect" title="RFID">RFID</a> tagging.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Finland" title="Finland">Finland</a> has the highest number of registered book borrowers per capita in the world. Over half of Finland's population are registered borrowers.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>25<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In the US, public library users have borrowed on average roughly 15 books per user per year from 1856 to 1978. From 1978 to 2004, book circulation per user declined approximately 50%. The growth of audiovisuals circulation, estimated at 25% of total circulation in 2004, accounts for about half of this decline.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>26<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Relationship_with_the_Internet">Relationship with the Internet</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Relationship with the Internet"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Howard_Tilton_Library_Computers_2010.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Howard_Tilton_Library_Computers_2010.jpg/250px-Howard_Tilton_Library_Computers_2010.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="166" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Howard_Tilton_Library_Computers_2010.jpg/500px-Howard_Tilton_Library_Computers_2010.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="4256" data-file-height="2832" /></a><figcaption>Library patrons using the Internet at Howard Tilton Library at <a href="/wiki/Tulane_University" title="Tulane University">Tulane University</a> in <a href="/wiki/New_Orleans" title="New Orleans">New Orleans</a>, Louisiana</figcaption></figure>
<p>A library may make use of the <a href="/wiki/Internet" title="Internet">Internet</a> in a number of ways, from creating its own <a href="/wiki/Library_website" title="Library website">library website</a> to making the contents of its <a href="/wiki/Online_public_access_catalog" title="Online public access catalog">catalogues searchable online</a>. Some specialised search engines such as <a href="/wiki/Google_Scholar" title="Google Scholar">Google Scholar</a> offer a way to facilitate searching for academic resources such as journal articles and research papers. The <a href="/wiki/OCLC" title="OCLC">Online Computer Library Center</a> allows anyone to search the world's largest repository of library records through its <a href="/wiki/WorldCat" title="WorldCat">WorldCat</a> online database.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Grossman(2009)&#95;28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Grossman(2009)-28"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>27<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Websites such as <a href="/wiki/LibraryThing" title="LibraryThing">LibraryThing</a> and <a href="/wiki/Amazon.com" class="mw-redirect" title="Amazon.com">Amazon</a> provide abstracts, reviews, and recommendations of books.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Grossman(2009)&#95;28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Grossman(2009)-28"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>27<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Libraries provide computers and Internet access to allow people to search for information online.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>28<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Online information access is particularly attractive to younger library users.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>29<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>30<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>31<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>32<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-MLA,&#95;DCMS,&#95;LASER(2006)&#95;34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MLA,_DCMS,_LASER(2006)-34"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>33<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Digitizing" class="mw-redirect" title="Digitizing">Digitization</a> of books, particularly those that are <a href="/wiki/Out_of_print" title="Out of print">out-of-print</a>, in projects such as <a href="/wiki/Google_Books" title="Google Books">Google Books</a> provides resources for library and other online users. Due to their holdings of valuable material, some libraries are important partners for search engines such as <a href="/wiki/Google" title="Google">Google</a> in realizing the potential of such projects and have received reciprocal benefits in cases where they have negotiated effectively.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Darnton(2009)&#95;35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Darnton(2009)-35"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>34<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> As the prominence of and reliance on the Internet has grown, library services have moved the emphasis from mainly providing print resources to providing more computers and more <a href="/wiki/Internet_access" title="Internet access">Internet access</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>35<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Libraries face a number of challenges in adapting to new ways of <a href="/wiki/Information_seeking" title="Information seeking">information seeking</a> that may stress convenience over quality,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>36<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> reducing the priority of <a href="/wiki/Information_literacy" title="Information literacy">information literacy</a> skills.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Bell(2005)&#95;38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bell(2005)-38"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>37<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The potential decline in library usage, particularly <a href="/wiki/Reference_desk" title="Reference desk">reference services</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>38<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> puts the necessity for these services in doubt.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Fukuchiyama_Public_Library_2F_ac_(5).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Fukuchiyama_Public_Library_2F_ac_%285%29.jpg/250px-Fukuchiyama_Public_Library_2F_ac_%285%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="166" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Fukuchiyama_Public_Library_2F_ac_%285%29.jpg/500px-Fukuchiyama_Public_Library_2F_ac_%285%29.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="4914" data-file-height="3267" /></a><figcaption>Library <a href="/wiki/Carrel_desk" title="Carrel desk">carrel desk</a> at Fukuchiyama Public Library in <a href="/wiki/Fukuchiyama,_Kyoto" title="Fukuchiyama, Kyoto">Fukuchiyama</a>, Japan</figcaption></figure>
<p>Library scholars have acknowledged that libraries need to address the ways that they market their services if they are to compete with the Internet and mitigate the risk of losing users.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>39<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> This includes promoting the information literacy skills training considered vital across the library profession.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Bell(2005)&#95;38-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bell(2005)-38"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>37<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-CILIP(2010)&#95;41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CILIP(2010)-41"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>40<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Kenney(2004)&#95;42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kenney(2004)-42"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>41<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Many US-based research librarians rely on the <a href="/wiki/Association_of_College_and_Research_Libraries" class="mw-redirect" title="Association of College and Research Libraries">ACRL</a> Framework for Information Literacy in order to guide students and faculty in research.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>42<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> However, marketing of services has to be adequately supported financially in order to be successful. This can be problematic for library services that are publicly funded and find it difficult to justify diverting tight funds to apparently peripheral areas such as branding and marketing.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>43<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Privacy" title="Privacy">privacy</a> aspect of library usage in the Internet age is a matter of growing concern and advocacy; privacy workshops are run by the <a href="/wiki/Library_Freedom_Project" title="Library Freedom Project">Library Freedom Project</a> which teach librarians about digital tools (such as the <a href="/wiki/Tor_(network)" title="Tor (network)">Tor network</a>) to thwart mass surveillance.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>44<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>45<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>46<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Librarians">Librarians</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Librarians"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Education_for_librarianship" title="Education for librarianship">Education for librarianship</a></div>
<p>Libraries are usually staffed by a combination of professionally trained librarians, paraprofessional staff sometimes called <a href="/wiki/Library_technician" title="Library technician">library technicians</a>, and support staff. Some topics related to the <a href="/wiki/Education_for_librarianship" title="Education for librarianship">education of librarians</a> and allied staff include accessibility of the collection, acquisition of materials, arrangement and finding tools, the book trade, the influence of the physical properties of the different writing materials, language distribution, role in education, rates of literacy, budgets, staffing, libraries for specially targeted audiences, architectural merit, patterns of usage, the role of libraries in a nation's cultural heritage, and the role of government, church, or private sponsorship. Since the 1960s, issues of computerization and digitization have arisen.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-britannica&#95;3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-britannica-3"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>3<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Types">Types</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Types"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:W.P.A._Packhorse_Libraries.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/W.P.A._Packhorse_Libraries.png/250px-W.P.A._Packhorse_Libraries.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="196" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/W.P.A._Packhorse_Libraries.png/500px-W.P.A._Packhorse_Libraries.png 1.5x" data-file-width="1384" data-file-height="1086" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Pack_Horse_Library_Project" title="Pack Horse Library Project">W.P.A. Packhorse Libraries</a>, Book Carriers ready to take the trail from <a href="/wiki/Hindman,_Kentucky" title="Hindman, Kentucky">Hindman, Kentucky</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Many institutions make a distinction between a circulating or <a href="/wiki/Lending_library" title="Lending library">lending library</a>, where materials are expected and intended to be loaned to patrons, institutions, or other libraries, and a reference library where material is not lent out. Travelling libraries, such as the early horseback libraries of eastern Kentucky<sup id="cite&#95;ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>47<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Bookmobiles" class="mw-redirect" title="Bookmobiles">bookmobiles</a>, are generally of the lending type. Modern libraries are often a mixture of both, containing a general collection for circulation, and a reference collection which is restricted to the library premises. Also, increasingly, digital collections enable broader access to material that may not circulate in print, and enables libraries to expand their collections even without building a larger facility. Lamba (2019) reinforced this idea by observing that "today's libraries have become increasingly multi-disciplinary, collaborative and networked" and that applying <a href="/wiki/Web_2.0" title="Web 2.0">Web 2.0</a> tools to libraries would "not only connect the users with their community and enhance communication but will also help the librarians to promote their library's activities, services, and products to target both their actual and potential users".<sup id="cite&#95;ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>48<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Library_system">Library system</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Library system"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>A <b>library system</b> is a central organization created to manage and coordinate operations and services in or between different centers, buildings or <a href="/wiki/Library_branch" class="mw-redirect" title="Library branch">libraries branches</a> and library patrons. They use a <a href="/wiki/Library_classification" title="Library classification">library classification</a> to organize their volumes and nowadays also use an <a href="/wiki/Integrated_library_system" title="Integrated library system">Integrated library system</a> - an <a href="/wiki/Enterprise_resource_planning" title="Enterprise resource planning">enterprise resource planning</a> system for a library used to track items owned, orders made, bills paid, and patrons who have borrowed information resources.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>49<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Many counties, states and universities have developed their own library systems. For example, the <a href="/wiki/Arrowhead_Library_System_(Minnesota)" title="Arrowhead Library System (Minnesota)">Arrowhead Library System</a> is a geographically based library system, established in 1965 to serve seven counties in Northeastern <a href="/wiki/Minnesota" title="Minnesota">Minnesota</a> with 29 member libraries in the system. In 2012 it merged with the North Country Library Cooperative to become the state's first regional public and multitype library system. The <a href="/wiki/London_Public_Library" title="London Public Library">London Public Library</a> in <a href="/wiki/Canada" title="Canada">Canada</a> has 16 branches, and the <a href="/wiki/Helsinki_Metropolitan_Area_Libraries" title="Helsinki Metropolitan Area Libraries">Helsinki Metropolitan Area Libraries</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Finland" title="Finland">Finland</a>, has 63 libraries.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-iiiHelsinkiMetro&#95;51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-iiiHelsinkiMetro-51"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>50<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Some countries, such as <a href="/wiki/Venezuela" title="Venezuela">Venezuela</a>, have only one library system for the whole country; the <a href="/wiki/National_Library_of_Venezuela" title="National Library of Venezuela">National Library of Venezuela</a> has 685 branches.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Library_branch">Library branch</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Library branch"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:North_Portland_Branch_Public_Library,_Portland_-_DPLA_-_85b06e5685680f767b4ac7a00a3c4385.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/North_Portland_Branch_Public_Library%2C_Portland_-_DPLA_-_85b06e5685680f767b4ac7a00a3c4385.jpg/250px-North_Portland_Branch_Public_Library%2C_Portland_-_DPLA_-_85b06e5685680f767b4ac7a00a3c4385.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/North_Portland_Branch_Public_Library%2C_Portland_-_DPLA_-_85b06e5685680f767b4ac7a00a3c4385.jpg/500px-North_Portland_Branch_Public_Library%2C_Portland_-_DPLA_-_85b06e5685680f767b4ac7a00a3c4385.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1335" /></a><figcaption>North Portland Branch Library, <a href="/wiki/Portland,_Oregon" title="Portland, Oregon">Portland, Oregon</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>A <b>library branch</b>, <b>branch library</b> or <b>community library</b> is a local library that forms part of a larger <a href="/wiki/Library_system" class="mw-redirect" title="Library system">library system</a>, using the same <a href="/wiki/Library_classification" title="Library classification">library classification</a> for its catalogs and is interconnected with all the other branches of the system through an <a href="/wiki/Integrated_library_system" title="Integrated library system">integrated library system</a>.
</p><p>Some countries' municipalities have their own <a href="/wiki/Library_system" class="mw-redirect" title="Library system">library system</a>, such as London Public Library (Canada) with 16 library branches, <a href="/wiki/Helsinki_Metropolitan_Area_Libraries" title="Helsinki Metropolitan Area Libraries">Helsinki Metropolitan Area Libraries</a> (Finland) with 63 libraries,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-iiiHelsinkiMetro&#95;51-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-iiiHelsinkiMetro-51"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>50<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/National_Library_of_Venezuela" title="National Library of Venezuela">National Library of Venezuela</a> with 685 branches.
</p><p>Some popular library branches include <a href="/wiki/New_York_Public_Library_Main_Branch" title="New York Public Library Main Branch">New York Public Library Main Branch</a>, part of <a href="/wiki/New_York_Public_Library" title="New York Public Library">New York Public Library System</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther_King_Jr._Memorial_Library" title="Martin Luther King Jr. Memorial Library">Martin Luther King Jr. Memorial Library</a>, a branch of <a href="/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Public_Library" title="District of Columbia Public Library">District of Columbia Public Library System</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Academic_libraries">Academic libraries</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Academic libraries"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Academic_library" title="Academic library">Academic library</a></div><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Egyetemi_K%C3%B6nyvt%C3%A1r4.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Egyetemi_K%C3%B6nyvt%C3%A1r4.JPG/250px-Egyetemi_K%C3%B6nyvt%C3%A1r4.JPG" decoding="async" width="250" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Egyetemi_K%C3%B6nyvt%C3%A1r4.JPG/500px-Egyetemi_K%C3%B6nyvt%C3%A1r4.JPG 1.5x" data-file-width="3648" data-file-height="2432" /></a><figcaption>The University Library at <a href="/wiki/E%C3%B6tv%C3%B6s_Lor%C3%A1nd_University" title="Eötvös Loránd University">Eötvös Loránd University</a>, Budapest, Hungary.</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Academic_Libraries" class="mw-redirect" title="Academic Libraries">Academic libraries</a> are generally located on college and university campuses and primarily serve the students and faculty of that and other academic institutions. Some academic libraries, especially those at public institutions, are accessible to members of the general public in whole or in part. Library services are sometimes extended to the general public at a fee; some academic libraries create such services in order to enhance literacy levels in their communities.
</p><p>Academic libraries are libraries that are hosted in post-secondary educational institutions, such as colleges and universities. Their main functions are to provide support in research, consultancy and resource linkage for students and faculty of the educational institution. Academic libraries house current, reliable and relevant information resources spread through all the disciplines which serve to assuage the information requirements of students and faculty. In cases where not all books are housed some libraries have E-resources, where they subscribe for a given institution they are serving, in order to provide backups and additional information that is not practical to have available as hard copies. Furthermore, most libraries <a href="/wiki/Interlibrary_loan" title="Interlibrary loan">collaborate with other libraries</a> in exchange of books.
</p><p>Specific course-related resources are usually provided by the library, such as copies of textbooks and article readings held on 'reserve' (meaning that they are loaned out only on a short-term basis, usually a matter of hours). Some academic libraries provide resources not usually associated with libraries, such as the ability to check out laptop computers, web cameras, or scientific calculators.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Robartslibrary.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d3/Robartslibrary.jpg/250px-Robartslibrary.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="166" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d3/Robartslibrary.jpg/500px-Robartslibrary.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="3008" data-file-height="2000" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Robarts_Library" title="Robarts Library">Robarts Library</a> at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Toronto" title="University of Toronto">University of Toronto</a>, Canada.</figcaption></figure><p>Academic libraries offer workshops and courses outside of formal, graded coursework, which are meant to provide students with the tools necessary to succeed in their programs.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>51<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> These workshops may include help with citations, effective search techniques, journal databases, and electronic citation software. These workshops provide students with skills that can help them achieve success in their academic careers (and often, in their future occupations), which they may not learn inside the classroom.
</p><p>The academic library provides a quiet study space for students on campus; it may also provide group study space, such as meeting rooms. In North America, Europe, and other parts of the world, academic libraries are becoming increasingly digitally oriented. The library provides a "gateway" for students and researchers to access various resources, both print/physical and digital.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>52<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Academic institutions are subscribing to electronic journals databases, providing research and scholarly writing software, and usually provide computer workstations or computer labs for students to access journals, library search databases and portals, institutional electronic resources, Internet access, and course- or task-related software (i.e. word processing and spreadsheet software). Some academic libraries take on new roles, for instance, acting as an electronic repository for institutional scholarly research and academic knowledge, such as the collection and curation of digital copies of students' theses and dissertations.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>53<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Moreover, academic libraries are increasingly acting as publishers on their own on a not-for-profit basis, especially in the form of fully <a href="/wiki/Open_Access" class="mw-redirect" title="Open Access">Open Access</a> institutional publishers.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>55<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Children's_libraries"><span id="Children.27s_libraries"></span>Children's libraries</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Children&#039;s libraries"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Children's library" redirects here. For the collection of digitized books, see <a href="/wiki/Internet_Archive%27s_Children%27s_Library" class="mw-redirect" title="Internet Archive&#39;s Children&#39;s Library">Internet Archive's Children's Library</a>.</div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Kinderb%C3%BCcherei_Ludwigsburg.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Kinderb%C3%BCcherei_Ludwigsburg.jpg/250px-Kinderb%C3%BCcherei_Ludwigsburg.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="171" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Kinderb%C3%BCcherei_Ludwigsburg.jpg/500px-Kinderb%C3%BCcherei_Ludwigsburg.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="699" /></a><figcaption>Children's library section in the Ludwigsburg City Library.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Children's libraries are special collections of books intended for juvenile readers and usually kept in separate rooms of general public libraries.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>56<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Some children's libraries have entire floors or wings dedicated to them in bigger libraries while smaller ones may have a separate room or area for children. They are an educational agency seeking to acquaint the young with the world's literature and to cultivate a love for reading. Their work supplements that of the public schools.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>57<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>58<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Services commonly provided by public libraries may include storytelling sessions for infants, toddlers, preschool children, or after-school programs, all with an intention of developing early literacy skills and a love of books. One of the most popular programs offered in public libraries are summer reading programs for children, families, and adults.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>59<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Another popular reading program for children is PAWS TO READ or similar programs where children can read to certified therapy dogs. Since animals are a calming influence and there is no judgment, children learn confidence and a love of reading. Many states have these types of programs: parents need simply ask their librarian to see if it is available at their local library.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>60<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Media_libraries">Media libraries</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Media libraries"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>In some countries, a difference is made between libraries that hold <a href="/wiki/Multimedia" title="Multimedia">multimedia</a> materials like videos, such as movies and documentaries, and sound recordings, such as music and audio books, and libraries that hold physical books.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>61<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The French term <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">médiatheque</i></span> describes this sort of multimedia library<sup id="cite&#95;ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>62<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and came from a period in the history of French libraries during the 1970s.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>63<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Notable media libraries include <a href="/wiki/Sendai_Mediatheque" title="Sendai Mediatheque">Sendai Mediatheque</a> in Japan, and from within France the <a href="/wiki/M%C3%A9diath%C3%A8que_Musicale_Mahler" title="Médiathèque Musicale Mahler">Médiathèque Musicale Mahler</a>, and <a href="/wiki/M%C3%A9diath%C3%A8ques_de_Saint-%C3%89tienne" title="Médiathèques de Saint-Étienne">Médiathèques de Saint-Étienne</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="National_libraries">National libraries</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: National libraries"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/National_library" title="National library">National library</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:National_Library_of_Wales.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/National_Library_of_Wales.jpg/250px-National_Library_of_Wales.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="166" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/National_Library_of_Wales.jpg/500px-National_Library_of_Wales.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="530" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/National_Library_of_Wales" title="National Library of Wales">National Library of Wales</a>, <a href="/wiki/Aberystwyth" title="Aberystwyth">Aberystwyth</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>A <a href="/wiki/National_library" title="National library">national or state library</a> serves as a national repository of information, and has the right of <a href="/wiki/Legal_deposit" title="Legal deposit">legal deposit</a>, which is a legal requirement that publishers in the country need to deposit a copy of each publication with the library. Unlike a public library, a national library rarely allows citizens to borrow books. Often, their collections include numerous rare, valuable, or significant works. There are wider definitions of a national library, putting less emphasis on the <a href="/wiki/Institutional_repository" title="Institutional repository">repository</a> character.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>64<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>65<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The first national libraries had their origins in the royal collections of the sovereign or some other supreme body of the state.
</p><p>Many national libraries cooperate within the National Libraries Section of the <a href="/wiki/International_Federation_of_Library_Associations_and_Institutions" title="International Federation of Library Associations and Institutions">International Federation of Library Associations and Institutions</a> (IFLA) to discuss their common tasks, define and promote common standards, and carry out projects helping them to fulfill their duties. The national libraries of Europe participate in <a href="/wiki/European_Library" title="European Library">The European Library</a> which is a service of the Conference of European National Librarians (CENL).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>66<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Public_lending_libraries">Public lending libraries</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Public lending libraries"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Public_library" title="Public library">Public library</a></div><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ET_Amhara_asv2018-02_img120_Awra_Amba.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/ET_Amhara_asv2018-02_img120_Awra_Amba.jpg/250px-ET_Amhara_asv2018-02_img120_Awra_Amba.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/ET_Amhara_asv2018-02_img120_Awra_Amba.jpg/500px-ET_Amhara_asv2018-02_img120_Awra_Amba.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="7952" data-file-height="5304" /></a><figcaption>A community library in <a href="/wiki/Ethiopia" title="Ethiopia">Ethiopia</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>A public library provides services to the general public. If the library is part of a countywide library system, citizens with an active library card from around that county can use the library branches associated with the library system. A library can serve only their city, however, if they are not a member of the county public library system. Much of the materials located within a public library are available for borrowing. The library staff decides upon the number of items patrons are allowed to borrow, as well as the details of borrowing time allotted. Typically, libraries issue <a href="/wiki/Library_card" title="Library card">library cards</a> to community members wishing to borrow books. Often visitors to a city are able to obtain a public library card.
</p><p>Many public libraries also serve as community organizations that provide free services and events to the public, such as reading groups and toddler story time. For many communities, the library is a source of connection to a vast world, obtainable knowledge and understanding, and entertainment. According to a study by the <a href="/wiki/Pennsylvania_Library_Association" title="Pennsylvania Library Association">Pennsylvania Library Association</a>, public library services play a major role in fighting rising illiteracy rates among youths.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>67<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Public libraries are protected and funded by the public they serve.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:2015-07_k1_CDMX_3474.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/2015-07_k1_CDMX_3474.jpg/250px-2015-07_k1_CDMX_3474.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="150" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/2015-07_k1_CDMX_3474.jpg/330px-2015-07_k1_CDMX_3474.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/2015-07_k1_CDMX_3474.jpg/500px-2015-07_k1_CDMX_3474.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4000" data-file-height="3000" /></a><figcaption>Stacks of the <a href="/wiki/Biblioteca_Vasconcelos" title="Biblioteca Vasconcelos">José Vasconcelos Library</a> in Mexico City, Mexico.</figcaption></figure>
<p>As the number of books in libraries have steadily increased since their inception, the need for compact storage and access with adequate lighting has grown. The <i><a href="/wiki/Library_stack" title="Library stack">stack system</a></i> involves keeping a library's collection of books in a space separate from the reading room. This arrangement arose in the 19th century. Book stacks quickly evolved into a fairly standard form in which the cast iron and steel frameworks supporting the bookshelves also supported the floors, which often were built of translucent blocks to permit the passage of light (but were not transparent, for reasons of modesty). The introduction of <a href="/wiki/Electric_lights" class="mw-redirect" title="Electric lights">electric lights</a> had a huge impact on <a href="/wiki/Lighting_in_libraries" title="Lighting in libraries">lighting in libraries</a>. The use of glass floors was largely discontinued, though floors were still often composed of metal grating to allow air to circulate in multi-story stacks. As more space was needed, a method of moving shelves on tracks (compact shelving) was introduced to cut down on otherwise wasted aisle space.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Library_2.0" title="Library 2.0">Library 2.0</a>, a term coined in 2005, is the library's response to the challenge of Google and an attempt to meet the changing needs of users by using <a href="/wiki/Web_2.0" title="Web 2.0">Web 2.0</a> technology. Some of the aspects of Library 2.0 include, commenting, tagging, bookmarking, discussions, use of online social networks by libraries, <a href="/wiki/Plug-in_(computing)" title="Plug-in (computing)">plug-ins</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Software_widget" title="Software widget">widgets</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>68<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Inspired by Web 2.0, it is an attempt to make the library a more user-driven institution.
</p><p>Despite the importance ascribed to public libraries, their budgets are often cut by legislatures. In some cases, funding has dwindled so much that libraries have been forced to cut their hours and release employees.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>69<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Reference_libraries">Reference libraries<span class="anchor" id="Reference&#95;library"></span></h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Reference libraries"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:2011_library_reading_room_at_University_of_Ibadan_in_Oyo_Nigeria_5600401644.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/2011_library_reading_room_at_University_of_Ibadan_in_Oyo_Nigeria_5600401644.jpg/250px-2011_library_reading_room_at_University_of_Ibadan_in_Oyo_Nigeria_5600401644.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="140" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/2011_library_reading_room_at_University_of_Ibadan_in_Oyo_Nigeria_5600401644.jpg/500px-2011_library_reading_room_at_University_of_Ibadan_in_Oyo_Nigeria_5600401644.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="575" /></a><figcaption>Graduate and post-graduate reading room at the University of Ibadan, <a href="/wiki/Nigeria" title="Nigeria">Nigeria</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>A reference library does not lend books and other items; instead, they can only be read at the library itself. Typically, such libraries are used for research purposes, for example at a university. Some items at reference libraries may be historical and even unique. Many lending libraries contain a "reference section", which holds books, such as dictionaries, which are common reference books, and are therefore not lent out.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>70<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Such reference sections may be referred to as "reading rooms" or "study rooms", which may also include newspapers and periodicals.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>71<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> An example of a reading room is the Hazel H. Ransom Reading Room at the <a href="/wiki/Harry_Ransom_Center" title="Harry Ransom Center">Harry Ransom Center</a> of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Texas_at_Austin" title="University of Texas at Austin">University of Texas at Austin</a>, which maintains the papers of literary agent <a href="/wiki/Audrey_Wood_(literary_agent)" title="Audrey Wood (literary agent)">Audrey Wood</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-ransom&#95;73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ransom-73"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>72<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Research_libraries">Research libraries<span class="anchor" id="Research&#95;library"></span></h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Research libraries"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Research_library" title="Research library">Research library</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Rijks_Museum_Library.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Rijks_Museum_Library.jpg/250px-Rijks_Museum_Library.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="163" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Rijks_Museum_Library.jpg/500px-Rijks_Museum_Library.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="8688" data-file-height="5680" /></a><figcaption>Main reading room of the <a href="/wiki/Rijksmuseum_Research_Library" title="Rijksmuseum Research Library">Rijksmuseum Research Library</a> is the largest public <a href="/wiki/Art_history" title="Art history">art history</a> <a href="/wiki/Research_library" title="Research library">research library</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Netherlands" title="Netherlands">Netherlands</a>. The library is part of the <a href="/wiki/Rijksmuseum" title="Rijksmuseum">Rijksmuseum</a> in <a href="/wiki/Amsterdam" title="Amsterdam">Amsterdam</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>A research library is a collection of materials on one or more subjects.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>73<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> A research library supports scholarly or scientific research and will generally include <a href="/wiki/Primary_source" title="Primary source">primary</a> as well as <a href="/wiki/Secondary_source" title="Secondary source">secondary sources</a>; it will maintain permanent collections and attempt to provide access to all necessary materials. A research library is most often an <a href="/wiki/Academic_library" title="Academic library">academic</a> or <a href="/wiki/National_library" title="National library">national library</a>, but a large <a href="/wiki/Special_library" title="Special library">special library</a> may have a research library within its special field, and a very few of the largest public libraries also serve as research libraries. A large university library may be considered a research library; and in North America, such libraries may belong to the <a href="/wiki/Association_of_Research_Libraries" title="Association of Research Libraries">Association of Research Libraries</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>74<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In the United Kingdom, they may be members of <a href="/wiki/Research_Libraries_UK" title="Research Libraries UK">Research Libraries UK (RLUK)</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>75<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Particularly important collections in England may be <i><a href="/wiki/Designation_Scheme" title="Designation Scheme">designated</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Arts_Council_England" title="Arts Council England">Arts Council England</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>76<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>A research library can be either a reference library, which does not lend its holdings, or a lending library, which does lend all or some of its holdings. Some extremely large or traditional research libraries are entirely reference in this sense, lending none of their materials; most academic research libraries, at least in the US and the UK, now lend books, but not periodicals or other materials. Many research libraries are attached to a parent organization and may serve only members of that organization. Examples of research libraries include the <a href="/wiki/British_Library" title="British Library">British Library</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Bodleian_Library" title="Bodleian Library">Bodleian Library</a> at <a href="/wiki/University_of_Oxford" title="University of Oxford">Oxford University</a> and the <a href="/wiki/New_York_Public_Library_Main_Branch" title="New York Public Library Main Branch">New York Public Library Main Branch</a> on 42nd Street in Manhattan, <a href="/wiki/State_Public_Scientific_%26_Technological_Library" title="State Public Scientific &amp; Technological Library">State Public Scientific Technological Library of the Siberian Branch of the Russian Academy of Science</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>77<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>78<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Digital_libraries">Digital libraries</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Library&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Digital libraries"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Digital_library" title="Digital library">Digital library</a></div>
<p>Digital libraries are libraries that house digital resources, such as text, photographs, and audio. These are curated by digital librarians. In the 21st century, there has been increasing use of the internet to gather and retrieve data. The shift to digital libraries has greatly impacted the way people use physical libraries. Between 2002 and 2004, the average American academic library saw the overall number of transactions decline approximately 2.2%. The University of California Library System saw a 54% decline in circulation between 1991 and 2001 of 8,377,000 books to 3,832,000.
</p>
